I think we must maintain this tradition, and I am pleased that the discussion has shown that there is also a great consensus between the parties here in the National Council and that we all agree that we should stay on this path. I can therefore also announce that we are applying for a seat in the United Nations Human Rights Council from 2011 onwards, because we believe that we should set a further sign of commitment in this direction. [[I should like to say a few words on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party and the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats) and European Democrats. Korun and Dr. Rosenkranz.]] Of course, I would like to speak especially, also because it is a matter of agenda, of our commitment to the protection of civilians, including within the framework of the United Nations, where we have the Presidency in the Security Council this month. For us this has been a long-planned topic, with which preparation has already begun last year. In particular, we wanted to address this issue because we have seen that in recent years, especially as regards the African continent, more and more civilians – to hundreds of thousands – have been affected by these armed conflicts. We therefore wanted to put something on the way with a resolution, which remains – which is not only an opinion on an immediate event, but it tries to give the United Nations something permanent and groundbreaking for its policy in the future. This has been quite successful, and I am pleased that many have replicated it and that there is a great support for it. I can tell you, if you look at the content, you will actually find some innovations: One consequence is that the Secretary-General of the United Nations now needs to develop a concept, a strategy. This is quite positive. For the first time, attention has also been paid to the role of disabled people in armed conflicts, which is an important step forward, since this has so far been neglected. We have also launched a kind of blueprint for any future United Nations mission, which is also very important. In the future, the United Nations Security Council will have to deal with every mission on how to guarantee the protection of civilians wherever blue helmets occur on this earth. That, I believe, is really something that we can be proud of. I would particularly like to thank our employees in New York at this point. Our Head of Mission and his people have prepared and negotiated this perfectly, so that last Wednesday everyone in the Security Council really launched this resolution in 1894 by a unanimous decision. This is for us Austrians a very positive sign of a commitment that does us well as a whole! [[It was in favour of ÖVP and SPÖ, as well as of representatives of the FPÖ.]] Finally, I would like to point out the following: Since this last Wednesday, further meetings of the Security Council have taken place under our Presidency and this resolution has already been referred to. Other countries have taken this up and are already applying it in their speeches. This is an encouraging sign that one can accomplish something permanent that is focused on the future. I am therefore pleased that the High House also appreciates this commitment of Austria. – I thank you very much for your attention.

ParlaMint-AT_2009-11-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00046_d7e15192	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! High house! Wars between two or more states were once waged, and today most of the internal armed conflicts dominate the crisis regions. Regular armies and non-governmental, armed groups face each other, and in most of these conflicts, the fronts and the difference between civilians and fighters are difficult to recognize. This, of course, has a negative effect on compliance with the provisions restricting the means and methods of warfare, which are also evidenced by numerous civilian victims. But not only the blurred fronts are due to the serious violations of international and human rights. Conscious criminal acts against the civilian population, such as kidnappings, sexual violence and so on, are part of combat strategies. We've heard this today, but you can't say it often enough: As women and children in particular are affected by these fight strategies, they must also be particularly protected. Especially people with disabilities who are already severely disadvantaged have no protection at all in conflict regions. In particular, states such as Austria have a moral obligation to help those affected in dealing with conflict. In principle, while governments in the respective countries are responsible for protecting their civilian population and respecting human rights, they often do not have the necessary resources. That is why it is essential that these countries receive financial support from other countries, but these funds – and I think very important – must also be used in a controlled manner. Also, the existing emotions in these regions often do not provide competent diplomats for peace negotiations. Therefore, the independent countries are also required to intervene in support. The active involvement of women in these peace processes should be a matter of course in itself. Ladies and gentlemen, the present motion for a resolution, which includes, among other things, strengthening the role of Austria as a mediator, should also be mandated for each individual to step up his efforts to support human and international rights and to support humanitarian measures.

ParlaMint-AT_2009-11-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00046_d7e16789	Next speaker: Mr Ing. Lugar. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2009-11-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00046_d7e16810	Madam President. High house! Yes, we need new rules for the banks, we need more transparency, and above all we need less hair-driving speculation. We also need to take a look at Basel II. Basel II was originally designed to strengthen the risk awareness of banks. This means that a bank that lends to someone a loan that is not too creditable must deposit more equity for it. Many then came to the creative idea of not depositing these loans with more equity, but insure them with credit derivatives. These assurances have been reached around the world, so that the bank, which, on the one hand, assured itself, was in the back of the insurance for another bank and, in the end, the risk was not minimized, but even expanded. In the current financial crisis, we have experienced that some banks were too big to fail, that is, too big to fail. It was very consciously worked with its own size and with its own system relevance in order to also take taxpayers into the duty to save a company – it cost what it wants. That was the problem. By this certainty, by this morale, Hazard, by this confidence: No matter what happens – the state will catch me!, more and more risks have been received. This too-big-to-fail is already there, I would say for even two decades. You've known to these big banks, you can do what you want, and you won't end up doing anything. One has taken more and more risk: If it goes well, you deserve it well; if it goes badly, the taxpayer pays. And that's exactly what we're talking about now. And if Mr Pröll, in his speech on the “Location of the Nation”, says that from now on we have to think about whether this should be so for all time that banks can in any case rely on the state to save them, then that is a good approach from my point of view, because the taxpayer must not stand up for any stupidity that the banks soften, and if the owners of the banks know that in extreme cases, if something goes wrong, they also have to be held liable accordingly, they will also see that less risk will be taken. [[OBJECTIVES]] That is, I believe, also in our interest, and that is why we must stop these misdevelopments. I'm talking about all the banks here, I'm not taking out a bank here. I am concerned that in business days, more risk-consciousness will return, and more commitment to customers, that loans are not simply insured, and that ultimately no matter what risk the bank takes over, because the credit is guaranteed accordingly. When I see the international development that banks are increasingly concentrated, that more and more banks are being merged, I have to say that is exactly the wrong development. What we need is a small bank structure, where individual banks can very well fail, but not the whole system is at risk. That's why: We need small structures, we need a return to the original purpose of a bank, namely the provision of loans, the close contact with the borrowers as far as possible, without any means of insure, without any possibility of reselling this loan. That is, this good old banker, who knows exactly what it looks like to the credit quality of his client, who often accompanies the company for ten, 15, 20 years, knows exactly what things are, we need again, we need these structures again, and if necessary we also need to restore these structures by law if the banks are not voluntarily willing to do so.

It is a question of the structure of world trade and a question of subsidies for agricultural products that often ruin small farmers in poor countries and have no opportunity to score on regional markets with their local products. Similarly, speculation on commodity exchanges that raise food prices is a major problem, but it would be politically possible to deal with it. The question of competition for agricultural land, where we are confronted with the phenomenon of "land digging" as a relative new product in which rich countries or companies bought or leased land from poor countries, either to produce raw materials for agricultural fuels or feed or food for their own population, would also be politically addressed. The people living there will be taken away from farmland the possibilities for the production of food, it will undermine the food sovereignty of countries. This too is, as has been said, politically to get under control. It is obvious that we are able to feed the entire world population without using genetic engineering, without harmful huge monocultures, but also without a total pesticides overkill. It would be possible if we were to put more money into sustainable agricultural development. In conclusion, I am very sorry that the final report of the World Food Summit in Rome, which ended these days, does not contain any such concrete commitments, but only remains very non-binding. For example, once again we lack the human right to food, which has been legally recognized since 1976, to live, we do not act in the sense of a billion hungry people and do nothing in terms of structural change of agricultural, trade, social and development policies. We also have a lot to do in Austria. In spite of all this, however, I would like to thank the colleagues of the Foreign Office for this very good, very readable and very well structured report.

In order to maintain and shape financial scope for necessary future investment, public budgets must also act structurally in order to increase productivity and innovation effects. Higher efficiency will therefore also be a priority as well as necessary activities in terms of expenditure and revenue towards greater tax justice. This should be a major concern for all of us in the future. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2009-11-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00046_d7e21330	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to thank Mr Fichtenbauer for the grace that we are allowed to err in small points. The law, which is politically not very controversial, has in itself been thoroughly reviewed. There have been some objections to which we have been followed as far as possible and also after the discussion in committee. The one point of discussion was related to the right of opinion of the Statistical Council – as an amendment has already been made in the Committee – which facilitated the access of science to microdata, the issue of the cost estimate to be drawn up by the Statistical Council together with Statistics Austria was dealt with, as it were, with the support of Statistics Austria. The last point discussed more intensively in the Committee was the issue of publishing obligations. We have taken these objections seriously and have tried to achieve a compromise that also takes into account the legitimate interests of the contracting parties. These are not only internal bodies, but also external bodies, and the compromise that has been reached is precisely that the publication has to take place two months later. In other words, there is, as it were, a temporary advantage of the client, even if the external leader is. This is something which is considered to be useful by these people and, from our point of view, it makes sense for contracts to be awarded to Statistics Austria. So, overall, I think, we have found a good compromise there. The starting point was that we had to implement an EU regulation from 2008 because the census year was postponed from 2010 to 2011. A sample count was made in 2006 that showed that the new registrar law, which was created at the time, works well and works in practice. Overall, this leads to – and this is also something related to the previous item on the agenda – that administrative measures can be used to save relatively much money with legal measures, taking into account that the census cost €72 million in 2001. It was acceptable and already available in the Committee that these legislations will also be used to discuss the issue of maps and the issue of mother-language surveys again. I agree with what the President of the Constitutional Committee, Peter Wittmann, said that of course the countries have the opportunity to collect certain data. So the country of Carinthia could collect this data, if it wants, but, of course, it cannot - to give another example - collect the data for the Burgenland, where the topic of local maps has been solved for many years, but it could do the whole thing for Carinthia. We do not want to do it for a number of reasons. One thing is that there has already been some unpleasant experience on the subject. My goal or our goal is not to bring about a conflict, but to find a solution. [[Abg. Petzner: Orientation yourself to Kreisky!]] And if it is clear that the ethnic groups are deciding against it, then it cannot be assumed that the collection of the mother tongue would lead to a solution. The legal argument put forward by Mr Petzner simply contradicts what the Constitutional Court has said. Mr Zinggl has already mentioned this. The Constitutional Court has said that it is necessary to use only an enlarged statistical record and that this is sufficient to assess this issue. This can be read in all the decisions that have been made in recent years. After some cases are pending at the Constitutional Court, this issue will soon be decided again. It is not our intention that a collection of the mother tongue at this point should continue to fuel the conflict. ParlaMint-AT_2009-11-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00046_d7e22047	We now come to points 21 and 22 of the agenda on which the debate is being held under one. There was no oral reporting. Mrs Ablinger has spoken on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). Fixed speaking time: 3 minutes. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_2009-11-19-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00046_d7e22075	Ladies and gentlemen! Mr President. Mrs. Minister! Since there is only so little time available, I will only address one request from the FPÖ that calls for the creation of its own law on the protection of cultural goods. This request implies that there would be no adequate protection of cultural goods in Austria, and that is incorrect. The protection of Austrian cultural goods is regulated in the protection of monuments in the Hague Convention; as far as the theft of works of art is concerned, it is a matter of the respective owners; and the BMUKK assumes responsibility as far as the collection goods are concerned. Unlike Switzerland, Austria is a member of the EU and participates in the comprehensive cultural property protection system. In other words, there is a directive which provides that Member States must take precautions that unlawfully acquired and stolen cultural goods which have been transferred abroad are returned to their country of origin by means of the court proceedings, and there is also a regulation on export authorisation. The UNESCO Convention, which has been addressed, therefore only concerns the cultural goods which are illegally placed on the market from outside the EU in Austria, and there is now negotiations between BMJ and BMUKK. For all these reasons, we reject this request. [[Intrusion of the Abg. New builder.]] - You're not surprised? However, I would like to say something very briefly to the last meeting of the Committee on Culture: We have also discussed and discussed the art and cultural report there, and I believe that this report of art and culture gives a beautiful, comprehensive current account for the diverse cultural life and shows an impressive balance sheet of all these projects. The Federal Government's support policy focuses above all on participation and participation, and I think that is a very essential point, because it is a matter of culture. For example, I would like to quote Gerald Raunig and Therese Kaufmann, who say in their work about European cultural policies: “Small and medium-sized cultural initiatives and media could play an essential role in enabling a Europe that is radically participation-oriented. Cultural policy has a duty to contribute to transforming these initiatives into a heterogeneous landscape of European public." In this sense, ladies and gentlemen, cultural policy is democracy policy. I think that is central and essential, because who would not dare any more democracy? Thank you.
